Nick F
Your Honor, my client, Mr. Nicholas Farrar, communicated a clear and time-sensitive offer to Mr. Jacob Lebby on November 8, 2025. At approximately 7:07 p.m., Mr. Farrar stated that if Mr. Lebby sent a payment of $135 before 7:08 p.m., he would honor his request to participate in a proposed trip to New York City. Mr. Farrar also explicitly stated that any payment received after 7:08 p.m., or for less than the full amount of $135, would be considered forfeit and would not apply toward the trip. These conditions were communicated in advance, leaving no ambiguity about the timing or amount required to accept the offer.

Despite being fully informed of these terms, Mr. Lebby voluntarily sent $50 at approximately 7:08 p.m.—after the cutoff time and for less than the specified amount. By failing to meet the express terms of the offer, no contractual obligation was formed between the parties. The payment, made outside the agreed parameters, was therefore forfeited under the terms provided by Mr. Farrar. Furthermore, according to Cash App’s Terms of Service (Section 4: Peer-to-Peer Service), completed transactions are generally “final and non-reversible”, and Cash App “is not responsible for losses due to mistaken, fraudulent, or unauthorized transfers.” This means that once Mr. Lebby voluntarily sent the funds and the payment was completed, it became legally irreversible through the platform. Given these facts, there is no contractual, equitable, or statutory basis requiring Mr. Farrar to return the $50, as the payment was late, insufficient, and final under Cash App’s governing terms.
